You are acting as an independent **Managing Director and Senior Forensic Retail & Consumer Credit Accounting Auditor**. 
You have been provided with the complete, chronological working paper compiled during a section-by-section forensic capture of LEW Group's financial results announcement (14 sections).

Review the sequential evidence trail below. ## REQUIRED STRUCTURE OF YOUR AUDIT REPORT

Please format your response into the following four clear sections:
1. **Executive Forensic Audit Summary:** Concise synthesis of the group's true economic performance, balance sheet health, and key findings.
2. **Detailed Forensic Reasoning & Determinations:** Step-by-step walkthrough of each key retail junction (merchandise vs credit/other revenue, gross profit margin bridge, debtors book health & IFRS 9 ECL provisioning, store network capex and trading density, IFRS 16 lease liability structure, and cash conversion / dividend payout), citing specific notes, tables, and calculations.
3. **Audit of Common Surface-Reading Pitfalls:** Contrast your findings against the common errors made by naive readers.
4. **Final Forensic Verification Matrix:** A clean markdown summary table listing each Junction, the Raw Initial Claim, the Resolving Note/APM, and your Final Verified Determination.

The 2026 financial year was characterised by a 
rapidly evolving operating environment, shaped by 
geopolitical uncertainty, global economic volatility 
and continued pressure on household finances. While 
domestic macroeconomic conditions improved as the 
year progressed, these positive developments were 
largely offset by heightened instability in the global 
environment.
The economic landscape in the early part of the 
financial year was dominated by the US-led trade 
tariff war, while the outbreak of hostilities in the 
Middle East in February 2026 intensified global economic 
uncertainty, resulting in increased volatility in international energy 
and financial markets.
On the domestic front, improving macroeconomic conditions were 
supported by greater political stability within the Government of 
National Unity. Moderating inflation and lower lending rates 
provided some relief to consumers and contributed to early signs of 
a recovery in household spending.
However, high levels of household indebtedness continued to 
constrain discretionary spending despite the lower interest rate 
environment. Furthermore, South Africa’s unemployment rate 
remained stubbornly high at 32.7% in the first quarter of 2026, with 
approximately 8.1 million people unemployed. The Group’s core 
customer base in the lower- to middle-income segment remains 
particularly vulnerable to the weak labour market, while ongoing 
pressures within the agricultural sector have adversely affected 
employment in many rural communities where the Group has a 
significant store presence. 
The conflict in Iran and the Gulf states has resulted in sharply higher 
fuel prices and increased supply chain costs, which continue to 
place pressure on consumers and retailers, and are expected to 
delay the pace of economic recovery. 
Against this backdrop, the Group’s business model continued to 
demonstrate resilience, supported by the strength of its trading 
brands, differentiated merchandise ranges, customer loyalty and 
prudent credit management. The Group remains well positioned to 
gain market share and deliver sustainable long-term growth.
Creating shareholder value
In this severely constrained trading environment, the Group 
increased revenue by 11.1%, expanded both gross and operating 
profit margins, and grew the debtors book by 15.2%. This 
performance translated into an 18.3% increase in headline earnings 
per share to 1 753 cents. 
The Group achieved or exceeded six of its seven financial and 
operating targets, with the increase in operating costs being the 
only metric outside the targeted range. The board and management 
have reviewed the targets for the new financial year and increased 
the operating profit margin target to reflect the expected 
performance for the year ahead and over the medium term.
The Group continued its track record of shareholder value creation. 
The total dividend for the year was increased by 12.1% to 897 cents 
per share, more than double the dividend declared just three years 
ago. The dividend represents an attractive yield of 11.4% at the date 
of the declaration, with the yield averaging 10.7% over the past five 
years. The board has consistently maintained the dividend payout 
ratio at 55% or higher, reflecting our commitment to delivering 
sustainable and attractive returns to shareholder

### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section
*(No stored questions were supplied — this is the first captured section. Proceeding to footnote/qualification unpacking and new question generation.)*

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive
No formal footnote markers (`[^1]`, `*`, note references) appear in the supplied D002 evidence on pp. 7–8. However, several embedded qualifications and forward-looking claims require unpacking and verification:

- **"Increased revenue by 11.1%… expanded both gross and operating profit margins… grew the debtors book by 15.2%"** `[D002, physical PDF p. 7]` — No absolute revenue, gross margin %, or operating margin % figures are given here. The debtors book growth (15.2%) outpacing revenue growth (11.1%) is a critical credit-risk signal: it implies either (a) credit sales mix expansion, (b) slower collections, or (c) book inflation from arrears. Must be reconciled against debtor cost %, impairment allowance (ECL), and satisfactory-paid customer % in D004/D005/D006.
- **"18.3% increase in headline earnings per share to 1 753 cents"** `[D002, p. 7]` — Implies prior-year HEPS ≈ 1 482 cents (1 753 ÷ 1.183). No share count, WANS, or diluted HEPS given; buybacks (R2.3bn returned over 5 years includes repurchases) will affect comparability.
- **"Total dividend… increased by 12.1% to 897 cents per share"** `[D002, p. 7]` — Implied prior DPS ≈ 800 cents (897 ÷ 1.121). Implied payout ratio = 897 ÷ 1 753 = **51.2%**, which is *below* the stated "55% or higher" floor. This is a mathematical tension: either the payout ratio is calculated on a different earnings base (e.g., diluted HEPS, or attributable earnings rather than headline), or the "55% or higher" claim refers to a policy ceiling/historical average rather than the current year. **Flag as verification question.**
- **"Dividend represents an attractive yield of 11.4% at the date of the declaration"** `[D002, p. 7]` — Implies declaration-date share price ≈ 897 ÷ 0.114 ≈ **R78.68**. Useful for market-cap and valuation cross-checks; verify against D005/D006 share data.
- **"Six of its seven financial and operating targets… operating costs being the only metric outside the targeted range"** `[D002, p. 7]` — The seven targets and their actual values are not disclosed here. Must be located (likely D004 CFO Report) to assess cost growth vs revenue growth and the revised operating profit margin target.
- **"Operating profit margin target… increased… to reflect expected performance"** `[D002, p. 7]` — No quantum given. Forward-looking guidance; verify new target % in D004.
- **"Return on equity improved to 16.2% from 15.4%… from 8.7% three years ago"** `[D002, p. 8]` — No equity base disclosed. Verify opening/closing equity in D005 statement of changes in equity (p. 55 sighting).
- **Five-year cash flow summary** `[D002, p. 8]`: "R6.3bn cash after income tax and interest, R3.7bn invested in expanding debtors book, R2.3bn returned to shareholders (dividends + buybacks), R368m capex." — Capex of only R368m over five years is remarkably low relative to a store network with >10 900 employees; implies either heavily leased/franchised footprint or under-investment. Must reconcile against IFRS 16 right-of-use additions, store count changes, and refurbishment capex in D005/D006. Also note the R3.7bn debtors book investment is the dominant cash absorber — confirms credit-led growth model.
- **"More than 10 900 employees in South Africa and the neighbouring countries"** `[D002, p. 8]` — Confirms cross-border (Southern Africa) operations; segmental revenue split by geography must be verified in D004/D005.
- **"Mholi Shandu… director of Monarch Insurance Company Limited, the Group's insurance subsidiary"** `[D002, p. 8]` — Confirms an in-house insurance underwriting operation. This is material for revenue decomposition: credit life insurance premiums/underwriting profit may sit in "other/financial services revenue" and affect gross vs operating profit classification. Verify in D005/D006 segment notes.
- **Board diversity figures** `[D002, p. 8]`: black representation 44% (2025: 38%), female 33% (2025: 38%) — Governance only; no forecast impact, but note female representation *declined*.

### [FORENSIC EXTRACTION: CAPTURED TEXT & FINANCIAL TABLES]
--- Section D002 | original physical page 9 | text selection | Commentary ---
Total revenue passed the R10 billion milestone for the first time as the 
Group delivered a highly competitive financial performance, with headline 
earnings increasing by 18.4% to R909.4 million.
This performance is particularly noteworthy given the challenging 
operating environment. Global geopolitical tensions, ongoing conflicts, 
supply chain disruptions and exchange rate volatility continued to create 
uncertainty, while consumers faced pressure from constrained 
discretionary spending, elevated household indebtedness and persistently 
high unemployment.
Against this backdrop, the Group’s resilient business model, strategic 
focus and operational execution enabled it to deliver another year of 
strong growth and value creation.
Revenue exceeds R10 billion mark
Merchandise sales increased by 7.3% to R5.5 billion. After increasing by 
6.7% in the first half of the year, merchandise sales growth gained 
momentum to 7.8% in the second six months. 
Sales in the traditional retail segment, which accounted for 89.7% 
of sales, increased by 7.5%. The speciality segment, comprising 
predominantly of UFO, Real Beds and Bedzone, grew sales by 5.4%. 
Comparable store sales across all brands grew by 4.8%. 
The 146 stores outside South Africa, which represent 15% of the store 
base, grew sales by 6.9% and contributed 18.2% of Group merchandise 
sales.
The strong credit sales growth trend continued, with credit sales 
increasing by 9.6%, highlighting the resilience of the Group’s business 
model in the difficult consumer spending environment. Cash sales 
increased by 2.5%. Credit sales now account for 69.4% 
of total merchandise sales, compared to 68.0% last year.
Credit sales growth is being supported by the Group’s social media 
strategy. Sales generated through online and social media channels 
accounted for 11.1% of merchandise sales compared to 9.6% in the prior 
year. Active customer engagement drove a 21.2% increase in the Group’s 
Facebook following to 4.6 million.
Other revenue benefited from the strong credit sales growth in recent 
years and increased by 15.7%. Total revenue, comprising merchandise 
sales and other revenue, increased by 11.1% to R10.3 billion. 
The gross profit margin strengthened by 30 basis points to 43.7%, 
supported by the introduction of new merchandise ranges as well
 as the favourable movement in the Rand/US dollar exchange rate. 
The financial performance is covered in the Chief Financial Officer’s 
Report on pages 48 to 50.

--- Section D002 | original physical page 10 | text selection | Commentary ---
Strong growth in debtors book
The debtors book grew by 15.2% to R9.2 billion as the credit customer 
base increased by 11% through effective customer acquisition and retention 
strategies. 
The quality of the debtors portfolio remains sound, with satisfactory 
paying customers at 82.6% (2025: 83.5%). This translates into more than 
57 000 additional customers being classified as satisfactory paid, 
supporting repeat sales as further credit is extended to customers with
 a good payment record. 
Debtor costs increased by 9.8%, with debtor costs as a percentage of 
debtors at gross carrying value improving to 14.3% (2025: 15.0%). 
Improved collection strategies contributed to a 13.6% increase in instalment 
sales collections, with the collection rate ending the year at a solid 78.1% 
compared to 78.9% in the prior year.
Record store openings
The Group opened a record 58 net new stores,far exceeding the 
target of 40 new stores, and increased the store base to 976. 
This included 36 new outlets for the specialist bedding chain, 
Real Beds, which expanded its footprint to 52 stores. Other new 
stores were opened across Lewis (net 4), Best Home & Electric 
(net 14) and Beares (net 7), while three underperforming UFO 
stores were closed. 
Over the past three years, the Group has opened a net 136
new stores.
During the year, 100 stores across the portfolio were refurbished 
to ensure that stores remain modern and appealing to promote 
merchandise. Stores are refurbished on average every five years.
In the new financial year, we will continue to seek opportunities 
to acquire well located trading space to support our store 
expansion strategy, with plans to open 40 new stores, including 
25 traditional retail stores and 15 speciality bedding stores. 
Outlook
The war in the Middle East has led to global economic 
uncertainty and volatility in international energy markets which 
has resulted in record fuel prices. Inflationary pressure from 
higher fuel and transport costs will adversely impact domestic 
consumer spending. 
We enter the new financial year with a clear and well-defined 
strategy, a good quality debtors portfolio with the highest 
number of reservable customers in the Group’s history and a 
resilient balance sheet. 
In these uncertain times, our experienced executive management 
team remains focused on the disciplined execution of the Group’s 
strategic and operational priorities. We will continue to invest for 
long-term growth in the debtors book and store portfolio and 
remain well positioned to gain market share. Credit sales will be 
the primary driver of sales growth and we will continue to focus 
on affordability, maintaining good stock availability and 
introducing new exclusive merchandise ranges to support 
future growth.
Appreciation 
Thank you to our chairman Hilton Saven for his decisive leadership and to 
my fellow directors for their strategic guidance, wise counsel and diligent 
oversight of the Group’s affairs. I also extend my appreciation to our 
highly experienced executive team for their leadership of the business 
during this challenging trading period.
The management and staff at head office and the stores across southern 
Africa are committed to delivering outstanding service to our customers, 
and I thank everyone for their contribution to another successful year for 
the Group.
Johan Enslin
Chief executive officer

### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

- **Q1 [HIGH] Payout ratio: 897c DPS ÷ 1 753c HEPS = 51.2%, below stated "55% or higher" floor. Verify stated payout ratio and its denominator.**
  - **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D004)`
  - **Answer & evidence**: This section does not disclose DPS, HEPS, or a payout ratio. It does confirm headline earnings increased **18.4% to R909.4 million** `[D002, physical PDF p. 9]`, which is a different growth rate from the 18.3% HEPS growth cited in the earlier Chairman's Report — a minor tension worth reconciling (HEPS growth vs headline earnings growth can differ due to WANS/buybacks). The payout-ratio denominator question remains fully open for D004.

- **Q2 [HIGH] Debtors book +15.2% vs revenue +11.1%. Extract gross debtors, debtor costs %, ECL/impairment allowance %, satisfactory-paid customer %, collection rate %, write-offs.**
  - **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D004/D005)`
  - **Answer & evidence**: `[D002, physical PDF p. 10]` confirms:
    - Debtors book **+15.2% to R9.2 billion**; credit customer base **+11%**.

  1. **"Satisfactory paying customers at 82.6% (2025: 83.5%)"** `[p. 10]` — a **90 bps deterioration** is presented alongside the phrase "quality of the debtors portfolio remains sound." The claim that this "translates into more than 57 000 additional customers being classified as satisfactory paid" is a **gross-count claim, not a rate improvement** — the rate fell. Must be reconciled against absolute satisfactory-paid customer counts in D004/D005.
  2. **Collection rate 78.1% vs 78.9%** `[p. 10]` — described as "solid" but **down 80 bps YoY** while the book grew 15.2%. A falling collection rate on a fast-growing book is a leading indicator of future impairment; check ECL trajectory in D004/D005.
  3. **Debtor costs 14.3% of gross carrying value (2025: 15.0%)** `[p. 10]` — improvement claimed, but the **denominator (gross carrying debtors) grew 15.2%**, so the ratio improvement may be partly denominator-driven.   4. **Gross margin +30 bps to 43.7% attributed to "new merchandise ranges" and "favourable Rand/USD"** `[p. 9]` — no quantification of each driver, no promotional markdown or freight line. The favourable FX tailwind is a **translation/import-cost benefit that can reverse**; must be decomposed in the D004 margin bridge.
  5. **"Record 58 net new stores… far exceeding the target of 40"** `[p. 10]` — net openings include **3 UFO closures**; gross openings therefore higher. Store-count build (976) and the 40-store FY27 plan (25 traditional + 15 speciality) are the footprint baseline; trading space m² and trading density are **not disclosed here** — open for D004/D005.
  6. **"100 stores refurbished… on average every five years"** `[p. 10]` — implies a recurring refurbishment capex cycle (~20% of stores p.a.); must be checked against the R368m five-year capex figure (Q5) in D005.
  7. **"Highest number of reservable customers in the Group's history"** `[p. 10]` — qualitative; no number. Open for D004/D005.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

- **No footnote markers (`[^1]`, `*`, note references) are present in the supplied D002.1 or D002.2 evidence.** The D002.2 table has no footnote definitions attached in this capture. However, the capture warning states: *"The boundary crosses text. Enlarge the area to include full characters, headings and footnotes."* This means **footnote text may exist on p. 14 but was not captured** — I will not fabricate it. A verification question is raised below to check for any footnote definitions on p. 14 in a later capture.

- **Table integrity qualification (D002.2)**: The table is visibly corrupted by the area-capture boundary. The row labelled "Increase in operating costs (including insurance" is split, with the fragment "ment | service expenses)" appearing on a separate line — reconstructing as **"Increase in operating costs (including insurance service expenses)"**. This is a material qualification: the operating-cost growth metric (12.7% actual, 7–11% target) **includes insurance service expenses**, meaning the "operating costs" miss is not purely store/staff cost inflation — it is contaminated by insurance service expense movements (likely Monarch Insurance underwriting/claims). This must be verified against the D004/D005 cost breakdown.

- **Target vs actual framing qualification**: The table presents 2026 targets alongside 2026 actuals and 2027/medium-term targets. The 2026 targets are the *original* targets set a year ago, not restated. The fact that GP margin (43.7% vs 40–42%), operating margin (23.8% vs 16–20%), and satisfactory-paid (82.6% vs 78–82%) all **exceeded** target by wide margins raises a verification question: were 2026 targets set conservatively, or is there a definitional/accounting change (e.g., IFRS 17 insurance presentation, or reclassification of insurance service expenses) inflating margins? This must be tested in D004/D005.

- **Borrowings vs gearing qualification**: The table explicitly distinguishes **borrowings ratio excluding IFRS 16 lease liabilities (22.4%, target <25%)** from **gearing ratio including IFRS 16 lease liabilities (40.0%, target <50%)**. The ~17.6 percentage-point gap between the two is the IFRS 16 lease liability burden — material for forecasting debt service and covenant headroom. The 2027 targets *loosen* both (borrowings 25–30%, gearing 45–55%), implying management expects to **increase leverage** in FY27 — a key forecast input.

- **Strategic objectives (D002.1) — forward-looking claims requiring verification**: (i) "Increase sales of higher-margin furniture and appliance product categories" — implies GP margin mix shift upward, but 2027 GP target (40–42%) is *below* 2026 actual (43.7%), suggesting management expects margin compression; (ii) "Invest in local and imported stock... mitigate supply chain challenges" — implies inventory build and FX exposure; (iii) "Expand the credit call centre" and "increase customers paying instalments on monthly debit orders" — implies opex investment that may pressure the 7–11% operating cost target; (iv) "Seek shareholder approval... for renewal of the share repurchase mandate" — confirms buyback continuation, relevant to WANS/HEPS (Q8/Q12).

| Operating margin | 23.8% | 16–20% | 18–22% | 18–22% |
| Debtor costs as % of debtors at gross carrying value | 14.3% | 13–16% | 13–16% | 12–15% |

**FY27 store plan (D002.1, p. 14):** 25 new traditional stores + 15 new speciality bedding stores = **40 new stores**; **150 store refurbishments**; expand smaller-format Lewis footprint.

**Strategic priorities FY27 (D002.1, p. 14):** expand credit customer base; maintain collection rates (increase monthly debit-order payers, improve failed debit-order collections, expand credit call centre); exclusive merchandise ranges + higher-margin furniture/appliance mix; speciality segment profitability; capital management (debtors book growth, capital returns, share repurchase mandate renewal); ESG/B-BBEE.

**Derived/implied (for model):** Operating cost growth 12.7% vs revenue +11.1% → ~160 bps negative operating leverage in FY26. Gap between borrowings ratio (22.4%) and gearing (40.0%) ≈ 17.6pp = IFRS 16 lease liability burden. FY27 targets imply planned deleveraging reversal (borrowings 22.4% → 25–30%; gearing 40.0% → 45–55%).

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

**No footnote markers (`[^1]`, `*`, note references) appear in the supplied D002.3–D002.9 evidence.** All seven subsections are narrative risk-register commentary (Related Risks / Risk Mitigation bullets). No tables, no small-print qualifications, no footnote definitions to unpack. This is itself a finding: the risk register is entirely qualitative — no quantified risk exposure, no sensitivity, no probability/impact rating disclosed in this capture.

Qualitative qualifications that carry forecast implications (treated as embedded caveats, not footnotes):

- **D002.3 — "weak trading conditions impact both sales and collections"** [p. 16]: management explicitly links macro weakness to *both* revenue and debtor-cost lines. This is a two-sided forecast risk: it supports the observed satisfactory-paid decline (82.6% from 83.5%) and collection-rate decline (78.1% from 78.9%) captured earlier. Must be tested against D004/D005 debtor-cost trajectory.
- **D002.3 — "lower- to middle-income target market"** [p. 16]: confirms credit-book concentration in the most rate/unemployment-sensitive LSM cohort. Raises ECL staging risk (Stage 2 migration) — verify in D005 ECL note.
- **D002.4 — "strategically maintains higher stock levels when required"** [p. 16]: explicit admission of buffer-inventory working-capital drag. Directly relevant to inventory days and cash conversion — verify in D005.
- **D002.4 — "supplier development loans provided to local suppliers"** [p. 16]: a *new* balance-sheet item flagged — supplier development loans are an asset (likely in trade & other receivables or loans). Not previously captured. Must be located and sized in D005.
- **D002.5 — "Converting more customers to the debit order payment platform"** [p. 16]: implies collection-cost and payment-mix shift; debit-order penetration is a leading indicator of collection rate. Check if disclosed as a % in D003 p.45–46 or D004.
- **D002.6 — "Continued investment in organic growth, particularly in the debtors book"** [p. 16]: confirms debtors-book growth is *deliberate capital allocation*, not incidental. Supports the +15.2% book growth vs +11.1% revenue gap (Q2) as strategic, but raises the question of whether growth is in higher-risk cohorts.
- **D002.6 — "targeted ranges communicated to shareholders"** [p. 16]: cross-refers to the borrowings/gearing targets captured in D002.2 (Q27). No new numbers.
- **D002.7 — "Comprehensive cyber insurance coverage is maintained"** [p. 17]: insurance premium cost sits somewhere in opex — relevant to Q24 operating-cost composition (insurance service expenses).
- **D002.9 — "managing the impact of regulatory change on margins, profitability"** [p. 17]: flags regulatory cost as a margin driver but unquantified. National Credit Act / affordability regulation changes would hit the credit book — check D003 p.45–46 and D005 for any regulatory provision.

**Missing-footnote flag**: No footnote markers present, so no missing-definition issue in this capture. However, the D002.2 p.14 capture warning (Q33) remains — footnote definitions beneath the targets table were not confirmed.

High-quality debtors book
The quality of the Group’s debtors portfolio remains sound, with the book increasing by R1.2 billion to
R9.2 billion. Collection rates ended the year at 78.1% (2025: 78.9%), with satisfactory paying customers
at 82.6% (2025: 83.5%). Debtor costs as a percentage of debtors at gross carrying value improved to
14.3% from 15.0% in the prior year.
The debtors impairment provision as a percentage of debtors at gross carrying value slightly increased
to 37.7% from 37.2%.
The debtor performance is covered in the Chief Financial Officer’s Report on page  49.

--- Section D003 | original physical page 47 | text selection | Commentary ---
Credit offering
Lewis, Best Home & Electric, Beares, Real Beds and Bedzone offer credit 
contracts to a maximum term of 36 months.
The credit offering is supported by Monarch, the Group’s insurer, which 
offers a range of optional microinsurance products to customers 
purchasing merchandise on credit. Insurance cover is offered in the event of 
death, disability, retrenchment, and accidental loss of goods. Monarch is a 
licensed microinsurer registered with the Financial Sector Conduct 
Authority.
Credit risk management
Core to the Group’s business model is the central granting of credit. 
This ensures that credit risk policies are consistently applied and removes 
subjectivity in the credit-granting process. As a responsible credit provider, 
an important factor to consider when granting credit is the level of 
indebtedness of an applicant, as this impacts directly on their ability to pay 
instalments. A comprehensive affordability assessment is conducted for all 
credit applications which includes assessing Lewis’ data, credit bureau 
information as well as the customer’s priority living expenses.
Advanced credit-granting systems are used to assess the customer through 
the credit underwriting process as follows:
Credit scoring: Information is gathered on the customer from credit 
bureaus and third parties, including employers, and processed through 
credit scorecards. For new customers, application risk scorecards predict 
the risk based on the information from these external parties. For existing 
customers, behavioural scorecards assess the risk through predictive 
behaviour based mainly on the customer’s payment record with Lewis, 
taking account of credit bureau and third-party data.
Assessing customer affordability: Information is collected on the 
applicants’ income, expenses and current debt obligations. Lewis uses its 
own priority expense model as well as the National Credit Regulator’s 
expense table in determining the customer’s minimum living expense.
Credit limit: The applicants’ risk score determined by the scorecard, 
together with the expense assessment and outstanding obligations, are 
used to calculate a credit limit within the customer’s affordability level.
These credit-granting scorecards are used to determine the Group’s 
appetite for risk. In determining the acceptable level of risk, the potential 
loss is weighed up against the potential revenue using the predictive 
behavioural models inherent in the credit-granting system. The Group 
monitors any variances from the level of risk that has been adopted and 
refines the scorecards, as necessary.
Credit-granting compliance
When entering into credit agreements, customers are interviewed by the 
store manager and all the salient terms of the credit sale, as well as the 
benefits of the optional products and services, are explained to the 
customer.
Prior to completing the transaction, the store manager ensures that all 
customers engage with a compliance call centre agent, in one of the nine 
official languages of their choice. The purpose of the compliance call centre 
is to further improve compliance, transparency and oversight of the in-store 
sales and credit application process, as well as limit potential 
misunderstandings of the cost of credit, services offered and fees to be 
paid by customers. The call centre is housed at the Group’s head office and 
employs approximately 70 permanent staff, increasing to 150 agents during 
peak periods.
All calls are recorded and stored to protect the interests of the customer 
and Lewis Group. Only once the call centre agent has successfully 
completed the interview with the customer, will the transaction be 
approved by the call centre. Without this approval, no transaction exists, 
and the goods cannot be delivered or invoiced.
Instalment collections
Lewis operates a hybrid credit collection process, with both store-based 
follow-up and cash collections as well as customers paying monthly 
instalments via debit order.
The store-based collections model has proven to be highly effective as 
stores are located close to where customers live, work and shop. Store 
collection staff are often from the same community and can communicate 
in the customer’s preferred language, which benefits collection rates.
The debit order strategy further supports collections and has created more 
capacity for in-store staff to focus on following up with defaulting 
customers. 
Bad debts
Accounts in default are written off where the customer’s payment 
behaviour cannot be rehabilitated after all reasonable collection methods 
have been exhausted. Bad debts arise where the customer’s account 
balance has been written off. Write-offs predominantly occur in the 
following categories:
• Customers significantly in arrears
• Non-performing customers in terms of the business’ credit management 
practices
• Customers with out-of-term accounts
Refer to note 2 of the summary consolidated financial statements on pages 56 
to 58 for detail on the determination of the impairment provision and the 
calculation of contractual arrears, as well as the combined impairment and 
contractual arrears tables.

**Q20 [HIGH] — Capture pp. 45–46 credit-risk report: ageing buckets, ECL staging (Stage 1/2/3), coverage ratio, affordability criteria, write-off policy, satisfactory-paid and collection-rate definitions.**

- **Answer & evidence**: The supplied evidence covers pp. 46–47 only (p. 45 not captured). Resolved elements:
  - **Affordability criteria** `[D003, physical PDF p. 47]`: comprehensive affordability assessment per application — Lewis' own data, credit bureau information, customer priority living expenses; uses Lewis' priority expense model plus the National Credit Regulator's expense table for minimum living expense; credit limit set from scorecard risk score + expense assessment + outstanding obligations. Central granting removes store-level subjectivity. Scorecards: application risk scorecards (new customers) and behavioural scorecards (existing, based mainly on payment record with Lewis).
  - **Write-off policy** `[D003, physical PDF p. 47]`: accounts written off where payment behaviour cannot be rehabilitated after all reasonable collection methods exhausted; predominantly (i) customers significantly in arrears, (ii) non-performing customers per credit management practices, (iii) out-of-term accounts. No numeric ageing threshold (e.g. 90/120 days) stated here.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

No footnote markers (`[^1]`, `*`) appear in the supplied pp. 46–47 evidence. The consequential qualifications are embedded in the table and narrative:

- **"Debtors impairment provision as a percentage of gross debtors at carrying value" 37.7% (2025: 37.2%)** `[D003, p. 46]`: The label mixes "gross debtors" with "at carrying value" — internally ambiguous. Arithmetic confirms the denominator is gross carrying value (9 167), not net book. This is a very high coverage ratio; it implies net debtors ≈ R5 708m (9 167 − 3 459). Must be reconciled to the statement of financial position and note 2 ECL staging in D005.
- **"Debtor costs as a percentage of debtors at gross carrying value" improved to 14.3% from 15.0%** `[D003, p. 46]`: Check: 1 310 ÷ 9 167 = 14.29% ✓; 1 193 ÷ 7 955 = 15.00% ✓. But note the improvement is partly a denominator effect — gross debtors grew 15.2% while absolute debtor costs grew only 9.8% (1 310 vs 1 193). Net bad debts fell sharply to 8.8% from 10.6% — a large swing that needs explanation (recoveries? write-off timing? mix?).
- **"Collection rates from instalment sales" 78.1% vs "Total collections from instalment sales" R7 101m** `[D003, p. 46]`: Implied instalment sales base = 7 101 ÷ 0.781 ≈ R9 092m; prior year 6 253 ÷ 0.789 ≈ R7 925m (+14.7%). This implied base is close to but not equal to gross debtors — the definition of the collection-rate denominator must be confirmed (instalment sales raised in the year vs average book).
- **Credit sales as % of total sales 69.4% (2025: 68.0%) with credit sales +9.6%** `[D003, p. 46]`: Implied total sales growth ≈ 9.6% × (0.694/0.680 adjustment) — consistent with the D002 revenue growth of ~11.1% only if cash sales grew slower; cross-check against D004 revenue absolutes (Q4/Q23).
- **Credit application decline rate rose to 41.8% from 38.5%** `[D003, p. 46]`: A material tightening signal — more applications rejected, yet active customer base still +11.0%. Suggests either application volume growth or looser scorecard thresholds; must be reconciled with the "sound book" claim and the satisfactory-paid decline (82.6% from 83.5%).
- **"The debtor performance is covered in the Chief Financial Officer's Report on page 49"** `[D003, p. 46]`: Explicit cross-reference — D004 p. 49 is the primary source for debtor-cost trajectory and post-year-end collections (Q38).
- **"Refer to note 2 ... pages 56 to 58 for detail on the determination of the impairment provision and the calculation of contractual arrears, as well as the combined impairment and contractual arrears tables"** `[D003, p. 47]`: This is the definitive pointer for ageing buckets, ECL staging and arrears tables — target D005 note 2.
- **Monarch qualification** `[D003, p. 47]`: Monarch is a licensed microinsurer (FSCA) offering optional microinsurance (death, disability, retrenchment, accidental loss) to credit customers. Confirms insurance revenue is tied to the credit book — relevant to Q6/Q13 (revenue classification) and Q40 (insurance expense).

--- Section D004 | original physical page 49 | text selection | Commentary ---
This performance was supported by the continuing good 
quality of the debtors portfolio, which contributed to a 12.8% 
increase in operating profit to R1.3 billion.
Headline earnings increased by 18.4% to R909.4 million 
(2025: R768.2 million). Earnings per share increased by 13.0% 
to 1 646 cents (2025: 1 457 cents) and headline earnings per 
share by 18.3% to 1 753 cents (2025: 1 483 cents).

--- Section D004 | original physical page 49 | text selection | Commentary ---
The final dividend was increased by 12.0% to 560 cents per share (2025: 
500 cents per share), bringing the total dividend for the year to 897 cents 
per share (2025: 800 cents per share). This equates to an earnings payout 
ratio of 55.0% (2025: 55.3%), in line with the Group’s track record of 
consistently maintaining a payout ratio of 55% or higher. The Group 
continued to generate competitive returns to shareholders, with the return 
on equity strengthening to 16.2% from 15.4% in the prior year, and up 
significantly from the 8.7% reported in 2023. 
Over the past five years, the Group generated cash of R6.3 billion after 
income tax and interest, invested R3.7 billion in the debtors book and 
R368 million in capital projects, while returning R2.3 billion to shareholders 
in dividend payments (R1 466 million) and share repurchases (R843 million)

--- Section D004 | original physical page 50 | text selection | Commentary ---
Financial performance
The following review of the Group’s financial performance should be read together with the audited summary 
financial statements on pages 53 to 62.
Revenue
Merchandise sales increased by 7.3% to R5 452 million (2025: R5 081 million). Comparable store sales across all brands 
grew by 4.8%. 
Credit sales grew by 9.6% and cash sales by 2.5%. Credit sales accounted for 69.4% of total merchandise sales 
compared to 68.0% in the prior year.
Other revenue, consisting of effective interest income and ancillary services income as well as insurance revenue, 
benefited from the strong credit sales growth in recent years and increased by 15.7%.
Total revenue, comprising merchandise sales and other revenue, increased by 11.1% to R10 320 million 
(2025: R9 288 million).
Gross profit
The gross profit margin strengthened by 30 basis points to 43.7% (2025: 43.4%).
Operating costs
Operating cost growth of 9.6% was contained below revenue growth. Costs were impacted by performance and 
growth-related expenses, including the accelerated expansion of the store base. Operating costs as a percentage of 
revenue reduced to 34.0% (2025: 34.5%). Insurance revenue increased by 16.7% while insurance service expenses 
increased by 24.3%, impacted by a slightly higher claims ratio. Operating costs including insurance service expenses 
increased by 12.7%

Cash and capital management
The Group’s cash and capital management strategy is focused on investing in the longer-term growth of the business 
and returning capital to shareholders through dividend payments. 
Cash flow from operations increased by 19.9% to R1 119 million (2025: R933 million). 
The total dividend for the year amounted to R469 million (2025: R417 million). No share repurchases were 
undertaken during the yea

--- Section D004 | original physical page 51 | text selection | Commentary ---
The Group’s net borrowings totalled R1 231 million at year-end (2025: R880 million). The gearing ratio (including 
lease liabilities) increased to 40.0% (2025: 36.6%) and the borrowings ratio (gearing ratio, excluding lease liabilities) 
increased to 22.4% (2025: 17.3%). The higher borrowings relate mainly to the increased investment in the debtors 
book and store expansion. Both ratios are well within the board’s risk appetite and management’s guided ranges.
The Group’s balance sheet remains strong and the net asset value increased by 7.7% to R106.22 per share (2025: R98.64).

### [SECTION READING RESPONSE / DETERMINATION]
### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

**Q1 [HIGH] Payout ratio: 897c DPS ÷ 1 753c HEPS = 51.2%, below stated "55% or higher" floor. Verify stated payout ratio and denominator.**
- **Status**: `Contradicted / discrepancy found`
- **Answer & evidence**: The report states an "earnings payout ratio of 55.0% (2025: 55.3%)" [D004, physical PDF p. 49]. However, 897c DPS ÷ 1 753c HEPS = 51.2%, not 55.0%. The stated 55.0% implies a denominator of 897 ÷ 0.55 = **1 631c**, which is neither HEPS (1 753c) nor basic EPS (1 646c). This is a material reconciliation discrepancy. The denominator used by management is unidentified — possibly diluted HEPS, attributable earnings per share on a different share count, or an error. **Remains open for D005** to extract diluted WANS/diluted HEPS and attributable earnings to identify the 1 631c denominator.

**Q2 [HIGH] Debtors book +15.2% vs revenue +11.1%. Extract gross debtors, debtor costs absolute, ECL/impairment allowance %, satisfactory-paid absolute counts, collection rate %, write-offs.**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005)`
- **Answer & evidence**: [D004, physical PDF p. 50] Debtors book R9 167m (+15.2%); collections R7 101m (+13.6%); collection rate 78.1% (2025: 78.9%); contractual arrears R2 331m = 25.4% of gross carrying value (2025: 23.4%); debtor costs R1 310m (+9.8%) comprising net bad debts written off R810m (2025: R840m) and debtors impairment provision R500m (2025: R353m); debtor costs 14.3% of gross carrying value (2025: 15.0%); impairment provision 37.7% of gross carrying value (2025: 37.2%); satisfactory-paid 82.6% (2025: 83.5%). **Still open**: absolute satisfactory-paid customer counts, ageing buckets, ECL staging, coverage ratio per stage → D005 note 2.

**Q3 [HIGH] Absolute operating cost rand amount and composition (incl. insurance service expenses).**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: [D004, physical PDF p. 50] Operating costs including insurance service expenses = **R4 564m** (2025: R4 051m), +12.7%. Excluding insurance service expenses, operating cost growth was 9.6% and opex/revenue fell to 34.0% (2025: 34.5%). Insurance revenue +16.7%; insurance service expenses +24.3% (higher claims ratio). Segmental split: Traditional R4 243m (2025: R3 816m); Speciality R321m (2025: R235m). **Composition (store/staff/occupancy vs insurance) not itemised** — remains open for D005.

**Q4 [HIGH] Absolute operating profit, revenue denominator, merchandise vs other revenue absolutes.**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: [D004, physical PDF p. 50] Total revenue R10 320m (2025: R9 288m), +11.1%. Merchandise sales R5 452m (2025: R5 081m), +7.3%. **Implied other revenue = R10 320m − R5 452m = R4 868m** (2025: R9 288m − R5 081m = R4 207m), +15.7% — arithmetic checks. Operating profit R1 300m (2025: R1 152m), +12.8%; operating margin 23.8% (+110 bps). Operating profit before impairments/capital items R1 376m (2025: R1 171m). Impairments and capital items R76.2m (2025: R18.2m), mainly ROU asset impairment in speciality segment.

- **Answer & evidence**: [D004, physical PDF p. 50] Credit sales 69.4% of merchandise (2025: 68.0%); credit +9.6%, cash +2.5%. **Implied rand split**: credit sales = 5 452 × 69.4% = **R3 784m** (2025: 5 081 × 68.0% = R3 455m, +9.5% — checks); cash sales = 5 452 × 30.6% = **R1 668m** (2025: R1 626m, +2.6% — checks). **Key anomaly**: debtors book grew +15.2% vs credit sales +9.6% — a ~5.6pp gap indicating either slower collections, longer repayment terms, or credit sales recognition timing. This is a forecast risk flag.

**Q24 [HIGH] Operating cost growth 12.7% vs 7–11% target; metric "includes insurance service expenses": extract absolute opex, split store/staff/occupancy vs insurance service expenses; isolate Monarch insurance movement.**

Insurance revenue +16.7%; insurance service expenses +24.3% (higher claims ratio). **Store/staff/occupancy split and Monarch isolation not disclosed** — remains open for D005.

**Q25 [HIGH] 2026 actuals massively exceeded 2026 targets (GP 43.7% vs 40–42%; op margin 23.8% vs 16–20%): check whether targets restated or IFRS 17/insurance reclassification changed presentation; obtain 2025 comparatives on same basis.**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005)`
- **Answer & evidence**: [D004, physical PDF p. 51] The targets table shows **2026 Actual** vs **2027 Targets** vs **Medium-term Targets** — the 2026 target column is not shown, so the prior 2026 targets cannot be directly compared here. However, the 2027 targets (GP 40–42%, op margin 18–22%) are **below** 2026 actuals (43.7%, 23.8%), confirming management expects compression. The "insurance service expenses" terminology and "insurance revenue" line strongly suggest **IFRS 17 presentation**, which likely explains why 2026 actuals exceed the old (pre-IFRS 17) targets. **2025 comparatives on same basis**: revenue R9 288m, GP margin 43.4%, op margin 22.7% — these appear restated to the same basis. Remains open for D005 to confirm IFRS 17 adoption note.

**Q26 [HIGH] 2027 GP margin target 40–42% is BELOW 2026 actual 43.7%: obtain management gross margin bridge/guidance explaining ~170–370 bps compression.**
- **Status**: `Unresolved — no bridge/guidance provided in this section`

This is the single most important forecast input gap. Carry forward to D005 (and possibly re-read D002 strategy section).

**Q27 [HIGH] Borrowings ratio target loosens <25% (2026) to 25–30% (2027); gearing <50% to 45–55%: extract absolute borrowings, IFRS 16 lease liabilities, total equity; reconcile ~17.6pp gap between borrowings (22.4%) and gearing (40.0%).**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005)`
- **Answer & evidence**: [D004, physical PDF p. 51] Net borrowings R1 231m (2025: R880m). Gearing ratio (incl. lease liabilities) 40.0% (2025: 36.6%); borrowings ratio (excl. lease liabilities) 22.4% (2025: 17.3%). **Implied reconciliation**: if borrowings ratio = net borrowings ÷ equity = 22.4%, then equity = 1 231 ÷ 0.224 = **R5 496m implied**. Gearing 40.0% implies total debt (incl. leases) = 0.40 × 5 496 = **R2 198m**, so **implied IFRS 16 lease liabilities ≈ R2 198m − R1 231m = R967m**. The 17.6pp gap (40.0% − 22.4%) is therefore explained by ~R967m of lease liabilities. **Cross-check**: NAV R106.22/share × ~51.9m shares = R5 513m — consistent with R5 496m implied equity. Remains open for D005 to confirm absolute lease liabilities and equity from the statement of financial position.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

**Footnote 1 — `*` on 2025 collections figure** [D004, physical PDF p. 50]
- **Text**: "* Correction to prior year figure." (2025 collections from instalment sales shown as R6 253m*).
- **Implication**: The 2025 comparative collection rate of 78.9% is computed on a **restated** collections figure. This means the prior-year collection rate as originally reported may have differed. **Verification needed**: what was the originally reported 2025 collections figure and collection rate before correction? This affects the YoY trend read (is 78.1% vs 78.9% a genuine deterioration or partly a restatement artefact?). Target: D005 (prior-year comparatives) or D002 (if 2025 IAR figures are quoted).

**No other footnote markers** (`[^1]`, `[^2]`, `*`) appear in the D004 pp. 49–51 evidence. The targets table [p. 51] and capital ratios table [p. 51] carry **no footnotes** — notably, the "borrowings ratio" and "gearing ratio" definitions are given inline in the commentary (p. 51) rather than footnoted, which is helpful but means the **denominator (equity) is not explicitly stated** — see Q27.

**Embedded qualification — "Operating costs including insurance service expenses increased by 12.7%"** [D004, physical PDF p. 50]
- This is a **dual-basis disclosure**: opex growth is 9.6% excluding insurance service expenses but 12.7% including them. The 2027 target (7–11%) is stated on the **including** basis. This means the forecast must model insurance service expenses separately — they grew 24.3% vs insurance revenue +16.7%, a **negative underwriting margin trend** (claims ratio rising). If this persists, it is a structural drag on the 7–11% opex target. Target: D005 (insurance/IFRS 17 note) to isolate Monarch.

**Embedded qualification — "Impairments and capital items totalled R76.2 million ... relating mainly to the impairment of right-of-use assets in the speciality segment"** [D004, physical PDF p. 50]
- This explains the **speciality segment operating loss of R(53)m** vs +R8m prior year. The speciality segment's operating profit before impairments was +R20m (2025: +R26m) — so the entire loss is driven by the ROU impairment. **Forecast implication**: speciality store expansion (+33 stores) is loss-making at the EBIT line; the ROU impairment suggests store closures or underperformance in that segment. Target: D005 (PPE/ROU note, lease note) to confirm impairment detail and whether further closures are planned.

**Embedded qualification — "The higher borrowings relate mainly to the increased investment in the debtors book and store expansion"** [D004, physical PDF p. 51]
- Net borrowings rose R351m (R1 231m vs R880m) while the debtors book rose R1 212m (R9 167m vs R7 955m). The debtors book growth alone exceeds the borrowings increase — meaning **working capital was partly funded internally**, but the debtors book is now the dominant balance sheet item and the key driver of gearing. **Forecast implication**: if debtors book growth continues at ~15% p.a., borrowings will keep rising; the guided loosening of the borrowings ratio to 25–30% is consistent with this.

**Embedded qualification — "No share repurchases were undertaken during the year"** [D004, physical PDF p. 51]
- Contrasts with the five-year R843m repurchase history. **Forecast implication**: FY27 EPS/HEPS growth will not benefit from buyback-driven share count reduction unless the AGM mandate is used. The 51.2% vs 55.0% payout discrepancy (Q1) cannot be explained by buybacks in the current year.

### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

- **Q1 / Q54 [HIGH] Payout ratio denominator (55.0% stated vs 897c ÷ 1 753.4c HEPS = 51.2%)**:
  - **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005)`
  - **Answer & evidence**: D004 p.52 confirms DPS 897.0c and HEPS 1 753.4c, so the arithmetic tension persists (897.0 ÷ 1 753.4 = 51.16%, not 55.0%). The implied denominator is 897.0 ÷ 0.550 = **1 630.9c**, which matches neither HEPS (1 753.4c) nor EPS (1 646.2c). Note EPS 1 646.2c gives 54.5%, still not 55.0%. The 55.0% denominator remains unidentified — must be resolved from D005 (diluted HEPS, attributable earnings, or a different share base). [D004, p. 52]

- **Q7 [MED] ROE 16.2% (2025: 15.4%) — recompute from equity**:
  - **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005)`
  - **Answer & evidence**: D004 p.52 confirms ROE 16.2% and NAV/share 10 621.5c (2025: 9 864.4c). With 51 646k shares in issue, implied NAV ≈ R5 486m (51.646m × R106.215). Using average equity (opening ≈R5 078m from 51.482m × R98.644; closing ≈R5 486m), average ≈R5 282m; 16.2% × R5 282m ≈ R856m attributable earnings — broadly consistent with HEPS × WANS. Exact equity and attributable earnings still to be extracted from D005 (statement of changes in equity, p.55). [D004, p. 52]

- **Q8 [MED] WANS / diluted WANS / EPS vs HEPS gap**:
  - **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005)`

Shares in issue 51 646k (2025: 51 482k). Implied WANS from EPS ≈ 51.87m; from HEPS ≈ 51.87m if HE ≈ R909m. Exact WANS, diluted WANS and treasury shares still required from D005. [D004, p. 52]

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

- **Footnote 1 — "All ratios are based on figures at the end of the period unless otherwise disclosed."** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: This is a material qualification. ROE (16.2%), ROCE (13.4%), and ROAA (15.3%) are stated as period-end ratios, not average-balance ratios — despite the labels "Return on **average** capital employed" and "Return on **average** assets managed." This means the denominators are closing balances, not averages. For forecasting, this affects comparability: if equity/debtors grow, period-end ratios will understate true average returns. Must verify in D005 whether the stated ROE uses closing or average equity — the label says "average" but footnote 1 says "end of period." **Verification question raised.**

- **Footnote 2 — Trading space unit "m[^2]"** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: The footnote marker `[^2]` appears on the trading space unit but **the corresponding footnote text is NOT present in this section capture**. This is a missing footnote definition — it may define whether trading space is gross lettable area, net trading area, or includes storage. **Cannot be resolved from this capture; verification question raised for D005/D006.**

- **Footnote 3 — "The 2023 figures were restated for IFRS 17."** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: The 2023 column (and by extension 2022 comparatives where relevant) is on a different accounting basis for insurance contracts. This affects: operating profit margin (2023: 13.7% restated), gross profit margin (2023: 40.6%), ROE (2023: 8.7%), and all ratios derived from insurance revenue/expenses. **Forecasting caveat**: the 2022→2023→2024 margin progression is not clean — the 2023 restatement may have moved insurance revenue/expenses between lines. Must confirm in D005 whether 2022 was also restated or left on pre-IFRS 17 basis.

- **Footnote 4 — "Foreign exchange gains and (losses) are included under operating costs from the 2025 financial year."** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: This is a **critical comparability break**. From FY2025, FX gains/losses sit in operating costs; before FY2025 they were presumably below operating profit or in a separate line. This means:
    - Operating profit margin 2026 (23.8%) and 2025 (22.7%) include FX volatility in opex; 2024 (14.8%) and earlier do not.
    - The opex increase (+12.7% in FY26) may partly reflect FX losses now flowing through operating costs — relevant to Q24/Q57 (opex above target).
    - Gross margin (43.7%) is likely unaffected (FX in opex, not COGS), but **must verify** whether import cost FX is in COGS or opex.
    - **Forecasting caveat**: FY27 opex forecasts must include an FX line; historical opex ratios pre-2025 are not comparable.

- **"2023 Restated" column header** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: Confirms the IFRS 17 restatement applies to the 2023 column specifically. The 2022 column is **not** marked restated — so 2022 vs 2023 comparisons cross an accounting basis change. This affects the five-year trend read on margins, ROE, and solvency ratios.

- **"Debtors impairment provision as a percentage of debtors at gross carrying value" (37.7%)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: The 37.7% provision ratio (2025: 37.2%; 2024: 37.5%) is remarkably stable across five years (36.0%–40.4%), despite gross debtors growing and satisfactory-paid deteriorating. This stability is suspicious — it may reflect:
    - A mechanical provisioning model (e.g., fixed % by ageing bucket) rather than forward-looking ECL;
    - Provision releases offsetting new impairments (consistent with impairment charge R500m vs write-offs R810m — net release of R310m);
    - **Forecasting caveat**: if the provision ratio is managed to stay ~37–38%, then P&L impairment charges may be smoothed, and the balance sheet provision may not fully reflect deteriorating collection rates (78.1%) and arrears (25.4%). Must verify ECL staging and coverage per stage in D005.

- **"Arrear instalments on satisfactory paid accounts as a percentage of debtors at gross carrying value" (10.5%)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: This is a **new metric not previously captured**. 10.5% of gross debtors are arrears on accounts still classified as "satisfactory paid" — i.e., customers who are technically paying but have arrear instalments. This is a leading indicator of future deterioration: satisfactory-paid 82.6% of customers, but 10.5% of the book value is in arrears even within that "good" cohort. Combined with 14.9% arrears on slow/non-performing, total arrears ≈25.4% of gross debtors (consistent with Q16's R2 331m). **Forecasting caveat**: the "satisfactory paid" label overstates book health; 10.5% arrear component within it is a hidden risk.

- **"Credit applications decline rate" (41.8%, up from 38.5%)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: Rising decline rate confirms tighter credit granting — this supports the improving bad-debt ratio (8.8%) but at the cost of lower credit sales growth (+9.6% vs debtors +15.2%). **Forecasting caveat**: if decline rate continues rising, credit sales growth will slow, pressuring revenue; if it stabilizes, bad debts may rise as the book seasons.

- **"Financing cover" (6.6×, up from 5.8×)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: Financing cover improved despite gearing rising (40.0% vs 36.6%) — implies EBITDA or cash flow grew faster than net debt. Consistent with net debt/EBITDA stable at 0.7×. **Forecasting caveat**: financing cover definition not stated (EBIT/interest? EBITDA/interest?); must verify in D005.

- **"Current ratio" jumped to 4.9× (2025: 2.6×)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: A near-doubling of the current ratio is a **major anomaly** not explained by the table. Possible causes: reclassification of debtors (current vs non-current), inventory build, or a change in short-term borrowings classification. **Forecasting caveat**: this must be reconciled in D005 — a 4.9× current ratio is unusually high for a retailer and may signal a classification change (e.g., debtors book split into current/non-current) rather than a genuine liquidity improvement.

- **"Cash conversion ratio" (62.5%)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: Definition not stated. Likely CFO ÷ EBITDA or CFO ÷ operating profit. At 62.5%, it implies significant working capital absorption — consistent with debtors book growth (+15.2%) outpacing credit sales (+9.6%). **Forecasting caveat**: must confirm definition in D005 to model working capital.

- **"Inventory turn" (4.0×, up from 3.8×)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: Inventory turn improving — implies inventory days ≈91 days (365 ÷ 4.0). This is a **new baseline** for the forecast model. Must confirm whether turn is COGS-based or sales-based in D005.

- **"Volume of shares traded" fell to 8.7m (2025: 14.2m)** [D004, p. 52]
  - **Implication**: Liquidity deterioration — traded volume down 39% YoY despite share price up 11.4%. Value traded R703.9m (2025: R873.9m). **Forecasting caveat**: lower liquidity may affect cost of equity and buyback feasibility.

### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

Only a subset of stored questions can be tested against the single income statement page (p. 54) supplied. Most D005-targeted questions (ageing, ECL staging, lease liabilities, inventory, definitions) require the notes on pp. 56–82, which are **not** in this capture.

- **Q4 / Q55 / Q13 [HIGH] — Revenue decomposition & GP presentation**
  - **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005 notes pp. 56–82)`
  - **Answer & evidence**: Revenue R10 319.9m (2025: R9 287.8m) decomposes as: Merchandise sales R5 452.1m; Ancillary services R1 106.0m; Effective interest income R2 383.4m; Insurance revenue R1 378.4m. Retail revenue R6 558.1m = merchandise R5 452.1m + ancillary R1 106.0m. Implied "other revenue" = R10 319.9m − R5 452.1m = R4 867.8m (matches D004's R4 868m), and this is now explicitly split into EIR R2 383.4m + insurance R1 378.4m + ancillary R1 106.0m. **GP presentation is now clear**: the statement is a single-step format — there is no "gross profit" line. Cost of merchandise sales R3 070.1m is deducted, then operating costs, debtor costs, and insurance service expenses are all deducted to reach operating profit before impairments R1 375.9m. So the D004 "gross margin 43.7%" must be computed as (Merchandise sales − Cost of merchandise sales) ÷ Merchandise sales = (5 452.1 − 3 070.1)/5 452.1 = **43.69%** — i.e. merchandise-only, excluding all financial services revenue. This resolves the "where does other revenue sit" question: it sits **below** the merchandise gross margin line, inside operating profit. [D005, p. 54]
  - **Remainder open**: split of ancillary services (initiation/monthly service fees vs other), and whether EIR/insurance revenue are presented net of any direct costs.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

The captured page contains **only note reference markers** (Notes 2.2, 3, 4, 5.3, 8, 9, 11) with **no footnote definitions printed on p. 54**. Per the workflow rule, I do not fabricate footnote text. What can be inferred and what must be verified:

- **Note 3 marker on "Retail revenue" R6 558.1m** `[D005, p. 54]`: The note reference sits against the *Retail revenue* subtotal, not against merchandise sales or ancillary services individually. This implies note 3 (likely "Revenue" segment/ disaggregation note) will define what constitutes retail revenue vs financial services revenue. **Verification needed**: does note 3 confirm retail revenue = merchandise + ancillary only, and does it disaggregate ancillary services (initiation fees, monthly service fees, other)? This matters because the 43.7% gross margin is merchandise-only; any reclassification of ancillary into merchandise would change the margin base.

- **Note 4 marker on "Cost of merchandise sales" R3 070.1m** `[D005, p. 54]`: Note 4 will contain the cost-of-sales composition — markdowns, supplier rebates, freight, FX, inventory obsolescence. **This is the single most important note for the gross margin bridge (Q14/Q56).** The 2027 target of 40–42% GP margin vs 43.7% actual implies 170–370 bps compression; note 4 is where the drivers (freight, FX, rebates) will be quantified.

- **Note 2.2 marker on "Debtor costs" R1 310.2m** `[D005, p. 54]`: Note 2.2 is the credit risk note. It must contain the ageing table, ECL staging, coverage ratios, write-off policy, and satisfactory-paid/collection definitions (Q45, Q46, Q48, Q53, Q72, Q73). The p. 54 split (bad debts R810.2m + provision movement R500.0m) is the entry point; note 2.2 is the destination.

- **Note 5.3 marker on "Interest expense" R224.7m and "Interest received" R22.2m** `[D005, p. 54]`: Note 5.3 must split lease finance charges (IFRS 16) from borrowings interest. This is critical for Q27/Q60/Q78 (implied lease liabilities ≈R967m) and for forecasting the interest line separately from lease liabilities.

- **Note 8 marker on "Impairments and capital items" R(76.2)m** `[D005, p. 54]`: D004 attributed the R76.2m to ROU impairment in the Speciality segment (Q61). Note 8 must confirm the nature (ROU vs PPE vs goodwill), the segment, and whether any of it is non-recurring. **Verification needed**: is the R76.2m entirely ROU impairment, and is it excluded from headline earnings?

- **Note 9 marker on EPS/Diluted EPS** `[D005, p. 54]`: Note 9 must contain the HEPS reconciliation (HE rand amount, WANS, diluted WANS, treasury shares) — the key to Q1 (55.0% payout denominator), Q8 (WANS gap), and Q12 (HE vs HEPS).

- **Note 11 marker on "Taxation" R281.1m** `[D005, p. 54]`: Effective tax rate = 281.1/1 134.9 = **24.77%** (2025: 228.8/983.7 = 23.26%). The ~150 bps increase is unexplained on this page; note 11 must reconcile to the 27% statutory rate and disclose any prior-year adjustments or non-deductible items. **Verification needed**: what drove the ETR up 150 bps, and is it sustainable for forecasting?

- **Structural qualification — single-step statement**: There is **no gross profit line**. Any forecast model must compute merchandise GP separately (merchandise sales − cost of merchandise sales) and treat EIR, insurance revenue, and ancillary services as separate revenue streams with their own cost bases (debtor costs against EIR; insurance service expenses against insurance revenue). Blending them into a single "gross margin" would misstate the economics. `[D005, p. 54]`

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

The supplied evidence contains **no footnote markers, no table notes, and no small-print qualifications**. The table is a clean OCI statement with two columns (2026 Audited Rm, 2025 Audited Rm). There is nothing to unpack on footnotes for this section.

Two structural observations from the table itself that matter for forecasting:

- **OCI is small relative to net profit**: OCI R17.5m vs net profit R853.8m (2.0%). Total comprehensive income R871.3m vs net profit R853.8m. For forecasting, OCI can be modelled as a small add-on (~2% of net profit) rather than a major driver, but the composition matters: the FVOCI debt instrument fair value adjustment (R22.6m gross, R16.5m net of tax) is the largest positive component and is market-driven, not operational. The foreign currency translation reserve is negative R(13.5)m (2025: R(8.7)m) — a widening FX drag, consistent with cross-border (Southern Africa) operations and relevant to the FX-in-opex question (Q70).
- **Retirement benefit remeasurements turned positive**: R14.5m net (2025: R1.7m) — a R12.8m swing. This is a non-cash actuarial item; it does not affect HEPS but does affect NAV and therefore ROE/NAV-per-share (Q65, Q68). It should be excluded from sustainable earnings forecasts.

No arithmetic tension is present in the table: net profit 853.8 + OCI 17.5 = 871.3, which ties to the stated total comprehensive income. The OCI sub-components sum correctly: 3.0 + 16.5 + (13.5) + 14.5 = 20.5; less tax effects (6.1) + (5.4) = (11.5) → 9.0; plus FVOCI gross 22.6 less tax 6.1 = 16.5 already included — the table's internal arithmetic is consistent with the stated OCI of 17.5 (the 3.0 "movement in other reserves" plus 16.5 FVOCI net plus (13.5) FX plus 14.5 retirement net less 5.4 tax on retirement = 15.1; the residual 2.4 is within rounding/other-reserve composition and is not a red flag at this level of disclosure).

### [SECTION READING RESPONSE / DETERMINATION]
### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

**Q7 / Q68 / Q92 [MED/HIGH] — ROE 16.2% basis; opening/closing total equity**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Total equity is R5 485.6m (2026) and R5 078.4m (2025) `[D005, physical PDF p. 55]`. Average equity = (5 485.6 + 5 078.4)/2 = R5 282.0m. ROE on average equity = 853.8/5 282.0 = **16.16% ≈ 16.2%** — matches the reported figure. ROE on closing equity = 853.8/5 485.6 = 15.6%; on opening = 16.8%. **Conclusion: ROE is computed on average equity**, contradicting footnote 1's "period-end" claim. Prior year: 754.9/avg(4 901.9?) — 2025 closing 5 078.4; 2025 ROE 15.4% implies opening 2025 equity ≈ 4 725m (754.9/0.154 × 2 − 5 078.4). Not verifiable here but consistent with average basis.

**Q15 / Q50 [HIGH] — Debtors gross R9 167m vs net carrying; reconcile to balance sheet**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for note 2.1)`
- **Answer & evidence**: Trade and other receivables on the balance sheet = **R5 876.1m** (2025: R5 162.0m) `[D005, p. 55]`. This is materially below the implied net debtors of R5 711m (9 167 − 3 456) computed from D004. The R5 876.1m line is "trade **and other** receivables" and includes supplier development loans, prepayments, etc. — so the pure net debtors book must be a subset. **Tension flagged**: implied net debtors R5 711m > total trade & other receivables R5 876.1m leaves only ~R165m for all other receivables — implausible if supplier development loans are material (Q36). Either the gross R9 167m / provision 37.7% basis differs, or the provision is not 37.7% of that gross. **Must resolve in note 2.1.**

**Q27 / Q60 / Q78 / Q91 [HIGH] — Net borrowings, lease liabilities, gearing 40.0% vs borrowings ratio 22.4%**
- **Status**: `Resolved (arithmetic reconciled)`
- **Answer & evidence** `[D005, p. 55]`:
  - Long-term interest-bearing borrowings: R1 350.1m (2025: nil)
  - Short-term interest-bearing borrowings: R41.6m (2025: R1 055.4m)
  - **Total borrowings = R1 391.7m**
  - Lease liabilities: R674.0m non-current + R289.6m current = **R963.6m** (matches the ≈R967m implied from D004)
  - Cash-on-hand and deposits: R161.2m
  - **Net borrowings = 1 391.7 − 161.2 = R1 230.5m ≈ R1 231m** ✓ (matches D004)
  - **Net borrowings + lease liabilities = 1 230.5 + 963.6 = R2 194.1m**; ÷ equity R5 485.6m = **40.0%** ✓ (this is the "gearing" definition — includes leases)
  - Borrowings ratio: 1 230.5/5 485.6 = **22.4%** ✓ (excludes leases)
  - **Conclusion**: gearing includes IFRS 16 lease liabilities; borrowings ratio excludes them. Both reconcile exactly. Note the **refinancing shift**: short-term borrowings fell R1 055.4m → R41.6m while long-term rose nil → R1 350.1m — a full term-out of debt, materially improving liquidity risk.

- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Equity R5 485.6m ÷ 51 646k shares = **R106.21/share** ✓ matches reported R106.22 `[D005, p. 55]`.

**Q71 / Q90 [HIGH] — Current ratio 4.9× vs 2.6×**

- **Answer & evidence** `[D005, p. 55]`: Current assets R7 385.6m ÷ current liabilities R1 502.4m = **4.92×** ✓ (2025: 6 634.4/2 507.7 = 2.65× ✓). **Driver identified**: the near-doubling is almost entirely the **R1 013.8m shift of interest-bearing borrowings from current to non-current** (short-term fell R1 055.4m → R41.6m). Current liabilities fell R1 005.3m; current assets rose R751.2m (mainly receivables +R714.1m). No operational anomaly — it is a debt-term reclassification.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

- **No footnote markers appear on this balance sheet page.** The "Notes" column references note numbers (2.1, 5.1, 6, 7) but the note text is **not present in this capture**. These are cross-references, not footnotes with commentary — no missing footnote text to flag here, but the referenced notes are the priority targets for the next capture.
- **"Audited" qualification on both columns** `[D005, p. 55]`: Both 2026 and 2025 are audited. This is the first hard confirmation that the 2025 comparative (IFRS 17 basis) is audited, resolving part of Q25/Q79.
- **Note 2.1 (Trade and other receivables) is the single most consequential missing note.** The face value R5 876.1m must be decomposed into: net debtors book, supplier development loans, prepayments, and other. This is the reconciliation hinge for the entire credit-book forecast (Q15, Q36, Q50, Q72).
- **Note 5.1 (Borrowings & cash)** must confirm the term structure, interest rates, and covenant terms behind the R1 350.1m long-term / R41.6m short-term split — critical for forecasting finance charges and refinancing risk.
- **Note 6 (Insurance investments) and Note 7 (Insurance contract asset)** are the balance-sheet anchors for the Monarch insurance economics (Q52, Q95).
- **Structural observation (not a footnote)**: The **R1 013.8m current→non-current debt reclassification** is the dominant balance-sheet event of the year and fully explains the current-ratio jump. Any forecast must model this as a one-off liquidity improvement, not a recurring working-capital trend.

### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

**Q67 [LOW] Total dividend R469m vs DPS 897c × 51 646k = R463.3m (~R6m gap).**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005 notes / D006)`
- **Answer & evidence**: The Statement of Changes in Equity shows "Distribution to shareholders" of **R435.8m** in FY2026 (2025: R310.2m) `[D005, p. 55]`. This is materially below both the R469m figure cited from D004 and the DPS-implied R463.3m. The R435.8m likely represents the *interim* dividend paid in cash during the year, with the *final* dividend declared after year-end (not yet a distribution). The R469m total dividend (interim + final declared) vs R435.8m cash distribution implies a final dividend of ~R33.2m declared but not yet paid at year-end. **Remainder open**: confirm interim vs final split, declaration dates, and shares entitled at each date in the dividend note.

**Q110 [MED] Statement of changes in equity: exact dividend paid and shares entitled at declaration dates; reconcile R469m vs R463.3m.**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005 dividend note)`
- **Answer & evidence**: Cash distribution to shareholders in FY2026 = **R435.8m** `[D005, p. 55]`. This does not equal the R469m total dividend cited in D004, nor the DPS-implied R463.3m. The gap (R469m − R435.8m = R33.2m) is consistent with a final dividend declared post-year-end and not yet paid. **Remainder open**: dividend note must confirm interim DPS, final DPS, declaration/payment dates, and shares in issue at each record date.

**Q64 [MED] No buybacks in year; treasury shares R(43.0)m (2025: R(37.6)m). Confirm authorization size, treasury share movement, post-year-end repurchases.**
- **Status**: `Partially resolved (remainder stays open for D005/D006)`
- **Answer & evidence**: Treasury share movement confirmed `[D005, p. 55]`: opening R(37.6)m, share awards to employees +R72.6m, cost of own shares acquired R(78.0)m, closing R(43.0)m. So the company **did acquire own shares** (R78.0m cost) during FY2026 — this is share purchase for share-scheme settlement, not necessarily a buyback. Share capital/premium shows no cost of own shares acquired in FY2026 (FY2025 had R43.9m cancelled). **Remainder open**: confirm whether R78.0m acquisitions are scheme-related or open-market buybacks; authorization size; post-year-end activity.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

This section (Statement of Changes in Equity, `[D005, p. 55]`) contains **no footnote markers, no table notes, and no small-print qualifications**. The only qualification is the column header **"Audited"** on both the 2026 and 2025 columns, which confirms the comparative figures are audited (relevant to Q25/Q79 — 2025 comparative is audited and on IFRS 17 basis, consistent with the balance sheet insurance contract asset R239.6m).

Key embedded qualifications to note for forecasting:
- **"Distribution to shareholders" R435.8m** is the *cash* distribution during the year, not the total dividend declared. The D004 total dividend of R469m implies a final dividend of ~R33.2m declared post-year-end and not yet paid. This matters for the dividend payout ratio and cash flow forecast — the FY2027 cash outflow will include this R33.2m plus the FY2027 interim.
- **"Share awards to employees" R72.6m** (2025: R46.8m) is a large non-cash charge to retained earnings, offset by treasury share movements. This is a real economic cost (dilution) that sits in the share-based payment reserve and flows through opex — relevant to Q3/Q24 opex composition.
- **"Cost of own shares acquired" R78.0m** (2025: R76.2m) — the company continues to buy its own shares to settle share awards. This is a cash outflow not visible in the dividend line; relevant to capital allocation and Q64.
- **Retirement benefit remeasurement +R14.5m** is an OCI credit (actuarial gain), not in headline earnings — one-off, should be excluded from sustainable earnings.
- **FVOCI fair value adjustment +R16.5m net** is an OCI item, not in headline earnings — but it affects the FVOCI reserve and the insurance investment portfolio value (R477.7m on balance sheet).
- **No hedge reserve or B-BBEE reserve separately visible** in other reserves — suggests either immaterial or embedded elsewhere; flag for Q44/Q112.
