1. BASIS OF REPORTING
The summary consolidated financial statements are prepared in accordance with the requirements of the 
JSE Limited Listings Requirements and the requirements of the Companies Act applicable to summary 
financial statements. The Listings Requirements require summary consolidated financial statements to be 
prepared in accordance with the framework concepts; and the measurement and recognition requirements of 
International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS®) Accounting Standards as issued by the International 
Accounting Standards Board (IASB®) and the SAICA Financial Reporting Guides as issued by the Accounting 
Practices Committee and Financial Pronouncements as issued by the Financial Reporting Standards Council 
and to also, as a minimum, contain the information required by IAS 34 Interim Financial Reporting.
The accounting policies applied in the preparation of the consolidated financial statements from which the 
summary consolidated financial statements were derived are consistent with those accounting policies applied 
in the preparation of the previous consolidated annual financial statements.
These financial statements are a summary of the Group’s audited annual financial statements for the year ended 
31 March 2026. The audited annual financial statements were prepared by the Group’s Finance Department 
under the supervision of Mr J Bestbier CA(SA). A copy of the full set of the audited annual financial statements 
is available on the Group’s website, www.lewisgroup.co.za.
These summary consolidated financial statements for the year ended 31 March 2026 have been audited by 
Ernst & Young Inc., who expressed an unmodified opinion thereon. The auditor also expressed an unmodified 
opinion on the annual financial statements from which these summary consolidated financial statements were 
derived. The audited financial statements and the auditor’s report thereon are available for inspection at the 
Company’s registered office and on the Group’s website, www.lewisgroup.co.za

--- Section D005.2 | original physical page 58 | text selection | Commentary | Note 2 ---
2. TRADE, OTHER RECEIVABLES AND DEBTOR COSTS CONTINUED
2.1 Trade and other receivables continued
Forward-looking information continued
High levels of uncertainty are affecting current global environments due to global wars, specifically 
in the Middle East, and geopolitical tensions which have resulted in significant price increases and 
supply disruptions in oil and other critical commodities. These pressures will flow into the South 
African economy through deteriorating terms of trade, exchange rate volatility, a constrained interest 
rate environment, slower economic growth and rising inflation, particularly food and transport costs 
which have a direct material impact on the Group’s customer base. The combined impact of the 
aforementioned will limit economic growth in the Group’s markets and place strain on the disposable 
income of the Group’s customers. Consequently, management has maintained a probability of 70% to 
the downside scenario, 25% to the base scenario and 5% to the upside scenario in its assessment of 
the forward‑looking overlay. As a result, the total forward-looking overlay amounts to R595.1 million 
(2025: R517.0 million).
Payment ratings
The customer’s payment profile is managed by using payment ratings. Payment ratings are determined 
on an individual customer level and measure the customer’s actual payments received over the lifetime 
of the account relative to the instalments due in terms of the contract. There are 13 payment ratings 
with customers being allocated to one of these 13 payment ratings in accordance with their payment 
behaviour. For the purpose of managing the business, the 13 payment ratings are summarised into 
three main groupings, namely:
• Satisfactory paid
These represent customers with a payment rating of between 9 and 13, with the lowest rated customers 
having paid an average of approximately 70% over the contract period.
• Slow payers
These represent customers with a payment rating of 7 and 8, with the lowest rated customers having 
paid an average of approximately 55% of amounts due over the contract period.
• Non-performing accounts
These represent customers not classified as Satisfactory paid or Slow payers with a payment rating 
of 6 and lower.

--- Section D005.2 | original physical page 59 | text selection | Commentary | Note 2 ---
2. TRADE, OTHER RECEIVABLES AND DEBTOR COSTS CONTINUED
2.1 Trade and other receivables continued
Interest rate risk
Interest rates charged to customers are fixed at the date the contract is entered into. Consequently, there 
is no cash flow interest rate risk associated with these contracts during the term of the contract.
The weighted average contractual interest rate on trade receivables is 23.9% (2025: 24.1%).
Fair value
In terms of paragraph 29(a) of IFRS 7, the carrying amounts reported in the balance sheet approximate 
fair value

“Bad debts before adjustment for interest on credit impaired accounts” is the gross carrying amounts of
the trade receivables written off. For credit impaired accounts, interest income is recognised by applying
the effective interest rate to the amortised cost (gross carrying value less impairment provision),
resulting in lower bad debts.

Non-performing = rating 6 and lower. 13 payment ratings total, measured as actual payments received over lifetime of account relative to instalments due. **Collection-rate definition (78.1%) still not located** — remains open.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

**Footnote 1 — Note 1 basis of reporting `[D005.1, physical PDF p. 56]`**
- Summary consolidated financial statements prepared under JSE Listings Requirements and Companies Act, with measurement/recognition per IFRS as issued by IASB, SAICA Financial Reporting Guides, and minimum IAS 34 content.
- **Accounting policies consistent with prior year** — no policy change flagged. This is important: it means the FY2025 comparatives (including the IFRS 17 insurance presentation) are on the same basis as FY2026, so YoY comparisons are clean.
- **Unmodified audit opinion** from Ernst & Young Inc. on both the summary and the full annual financial statements. This removes audit-qualification risk from the forecast base.
- Prepared under supervision of Mr J Bestbier CA(SA); full audited statements available on website. No going-concern or emphasis-of-matter qualification disclosed in this capture.

**Footnote * — Arrears restatement `[D005.2, physical PDF p. 58]`**
- "*Instalments in arrears for the comparative period have been restated to accurately allocate arrears between ≤3 months and >3 months for a discrete category of accounts. The impact of the reallocation is R187.8 million as at 31 March 2025.*"
- **What it changes**: Only the split between ≤3m and >3m arrears in the 2025 comparative. Total 2025 arrears R1 862.6m is unchanged. The R187.8m reallocation means the 2025 >3m arrears figure as originally reported was understated by up to R187.8m — so the YoY deterioration in >3m arrears (R1 180.3m → R1 540.9m, +30.6%) is on a restated, comparable basis. **Forecast implication**: the >3m bucket is the higher-risk cohort; its growth outpaces total arrears growth (+25.2%), confirming ageing deterioration.

**Footnote — Note 2.2 bad debts definition `[D005.2, physical PDF p. 59]`**
- "'Bad debts before adjustment for interest on credit impaired accounts' is the gross carrying amounts of the trade receivables written off. For credit impaired accounts, interest income is recognised by applying the effective interest rate to the amortised cost (gross carrying value less impairment provision), resulting in lower bad debts."
- **What it changes**: The R1 009.0m gross write-off is reduced by R104.7m of interest that would have been recognised on credit-impaired accounts, giving net bad debts R904.3m. This is an EIR mechanics point, not a cash item. **Forecast implication**: as the credit-impaired book grows, the interest adjustment grows (R101.0m → R104.7m, +3.7%), slightly flattering net bad debts relative to gross write-offs.

**Forward-looking overlay qualification `[D005.2, physical PDF p. 58]`**
- Overlay R595.1m (2025: R517.0m), +15.1% YoY. Scenario weightings **70% downside / 25% base / 5% upside** — heavily skewed to downside. Management explicitly cites Middle East wars, oil/commodity price increases, supply disruptions, exchange rate volatility, constrained interest rates, slower growth, and rising food/transport inflation as direct pressures on the customer base.
- **What it changes**: The overlay is a material component of the R3 458.6m provision (17.2% of total provision). The 70% downside weighting is a strong signal that management expects credit conditions to remain stressed — **forecast implication**: do not assume provision release; model provision as sticky or rising. The overlay growth (+R78.1m) is a leading indicator of expected deterioration.

**Interest rate risk qualification `[D005.2, physical PDF p. 59]`**
- Customer interest rates **fixed at contract date** — no cash flow interest rate risk during contract term. Weighted average contractual interest rate **23.9% (2025: 24.1%)** — 20 bps compression.
- **What it changes**: The debtors book is insulated from SARB repo moves on existing contracts, but new business is written at lower rates (24.1% → 23.9%), which compresses EIR revenue per rand of book. **Forecast implication**: EIR revenue growth will lag book growth unless volume accelerates; the 20 bps compression is a modest headwind to the R2 383.4m EIR line.

**Fair value qualification `[D005.2, physical PDF p. 59]`**
- Carrying amounts approximate fair value per IFRS 7 para 29(a). No fair value adjustment needed.

Total facilities include long-term revolving credit facilities and short-term overnight facilities (interest-
bearing borrowings). The interest rates on the overnight facilities are based on rates as determined by
each of the banks based on market conditions. The interest rates on the revolving credit facilities are
linked to three-month Johannesburg Interbank Average Rate (JIBAR).
The South African Reserve Bank has announced that the JIBAR will be gradually phased out and
replaced by the South African Overnight Index Average (ZARONIA) as the primary reference rate for all
rand-denominated floating rate borrowings. This transition aims to align South Africa with global
best practices for benchmark rates, improving transparency, reliability and robustness in financial
markets. Of the R1 billion revolving credit facilities, R700 million includes rate reform language for the
anticipated transition to  ZARONIA. The transition to ZARONIA for the remaining revolving credit facility
balance and the DMTN note is being actively managed. The Group does not foresee any material impact.
Domestic Medium-Term Note (DMTN) programme
The Group has established a DMTN programme under which notes of up to R2 billion can be issued.
On 30 September 2025, the Group issued a three-year, R500 million floating rate note through a private
placement. The bond was issued at an interest rate equivalent to three-month JIBAR plus a margin of
135 basis points and has a maturity date of 30 September 2028.
Interest rate profile
The weighted average interest rate applicable at the end of the reporting period was 8.3% (2025: 9.1%).

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

**Footnote [^1] — Note 8 "Impairments and capital items" [D005.3, p. 61]**
- **Text**: "This includes the before tax effect of all re-measurements and capital items excluded from headline earnings per share in accordance with the guidance contained in SAICA Circular 1/2023: Headline Earnings."
- **Implication**: The R76.2m total (ROU impairment R65.1m + trademark R10.4m + disposal loss R0.7m) is **excluded from HEPS**. This is the missing piece for Q12/Q88: HEPS (1 753.4c) is struck after adding back R76.2m pre-tax (≈R55.3m post-tax at 27.5% ETR) to net profit R853.8m. **Verification needed**: confirm the exact post-tax add-back and HE rand amount in Note 9 to reconcile HEPS 1 753.4c vs EPS 1 646.2c (difference 107.2c × ~51.87m WANS ≈ R55.6m — consistent with R76.2m × (1 − 27%) ≈ R55.6m). This is a strong arithmetic bridge but must be confirmed against Note 9.

**Footnote [^2] — Note 8 "Impairment of right-of-use assets" [D005.3, p. 61]**
- **Text**: "Refer note 7 in the full set of the audited annual financial statements."
- **Implication**: The ROU impairment detail (store-level, segment, discount rate, recoverable amount basis) is **NOT in this summary capture** — it sits in full AFS note 7. **Verification needed**: capture full AFS note 7 to confirm Speciality segment attribution, store closure plans, and whether further impairments are expected in FY27.

**Note 5.2 footnote — Borrowings ratio definition [D005.3, p. 60]**
- **Text**: "The borrowings ratio is calculated as net debt excluding lease liabilities, divided by equity capital."
- **Implication**: Resolves the gearing vs borrowings-ratio distinction (Q60/Q75). Gearing 40.0% = net debt R2 194.1m ÷ equity R5 485.6m (includes leases). Borrowings ratio 22.4% = net borrowings R1 230.5m ÷ equity R5 485.6m (excludes leases). **Arithmetic check**: 2 194.1/5 485.6 = 40.0% ✓; 1 230.5/5 485.6 = 22.4% ✓.

**Note 5.3 footnote (*) — "Other" interest [D005.3, p. 60]**
- **Text**: "For the current year, this amount primarily comprises a reversal of interest accrued in prior years."
- **Implication**: The +R9.2m "Other" interest income (vs −R9.6m prior year) is a **one-off prior-year accrual reversal**, not recurring. This flatters net finance costs by ~R9.2m in FY26 and creates a R18.8m YoY swing vs FY25. **Forecast adjustment**: normalise "Other" to ~nil for FY27; do not extrapolate the +R9.2m. Also explains why net finance costs fell only R2.1m despite borrowings interest rising R18.2m.

**Note 5.1 — JIBAR→ZARONIA qualification [D005.3, p. 60]**
- **Text**: "Of the R1 billion revolving credit facilities, R700 million includes rate reform language... The transition to ZARONIA for the remaining revolving credit facility balance and the DMTN note is being actively managed. The Group does not foresee any material impact."
- **Implication**: Management asserts no material impact, but **R800m of R1.35bn long-term borrowings (R300m RCF + R500m DMTN) lacks rate-reform language** — a latent refinancing/documentation risk. **Verification needed**: confirm in full AFS financial-risk note whether any basis-risk or transition cost is quantified.

**Note 7 — Insurance contract asset geography [D005.3, p. 61]**
- **Text**: "mainly relates to Customer Protection Insurance (CPI) contracts sold in South Africa, Botswana, Lesotho, Eswatini and Namibia."
- **Implication**: Confirms **cross-border insurance exposure** (4 non-SA markets). CPI is credit-linked — the R271.2m contract asset (asset for remaining coverage R351.8m less LIC R80.6m) is sensitive to credit sales volume and early settlement/cancellation. **Verification needed**: confirm CPI cancellation/refund experience and whether the R351.8m remaining-coverage asset amortises in line with credit book run-off.

**Note 6 — Fair value hierarchy qualification [D005.3, p. 61]**
- **Text**: "Insurance investments are valued with reference to observable market data on the JSE and are categorised under Level 2. Refer note 9.1 in the full set of the audited annual financial statements."
- **Implication**: All R477.7m insurance investments are Level 2 (no Level 3 judgement). Low valuation risk. **Verification needed**: note 9.1 in full AFS for any Level 3 reconciliation (none expected).

**Note 3 — Implicit qualification: no fee-type split [D005.3, p. 59]**
- **Observation**: Note 3 splits ancillary only by *timing* (point in time R265.8m / over time R840.2m), not by *fee type* (initiation vs monthly service vs other). This is a **disclosure gap** relative to the D004 narrative which referenced initiation fees and monthly service fees. **Verification needed**: full AFS revenue note (likely note 3 continued or note 24) for fee-type disaggregation.

**Note 4 — Implicit qualification: single-line cost of sales [D005.3, p. 59]**
- **Observation**: Cost of merchandise sales R3 070.1m is a single line with **no markdown/rebate/freight/FX/obsolescence bridge**. The 43.7% margin (up 30bps) cannot be decomposed from this summary. **Verification needed**: full AFS cost-of-sales note or D004 margin bridge.

12.5 Real Beds acquisition
In the prior year, the Group acquired the businesses trading under the Real Beds brand for a total
purchase consideration of R20.4 million effective 29 June 2024. An additional four stores were acquired
in Botswana with effect 1 November 2024, for a purchase consideration of R15.3 million. The acquisitions
had an immaterial impact on the financial results and financial position of the Group for the year ended
31 March 2025.
12.6 Lease liability payments
The total lease payments amount to R494.4 million (2025: R456.3 million), which includes the capital
portion of R328.4 million (2025: R296.7 million) reflected under financing activities, and the remaining
balance included in cash flow from trading.
13. POST BALANCE SHEET EVENTS
There were no significant post balance sheet events that occurred between the year end and the date of
approval of the financial statements by the directors.

### [SECTION READING RESPONSE / DETERMINATION]
### 1. Answers to Stored Questions for This Section

**Q1 [HIGH] Payout ratio 55.0% — implied denominator unidentified**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Note 9 [D005.4, p. 62] gives dividends declared per share 897.0c (interim 337.0c + final 560.0c). 897.0 ÷ 1 753.4 (HEPS) = 51.2%; ÷ 1 646.2 (EPS) = 54.5%; ÷ 1 690.4 (diluted HEPS) = 53.1%; ÷ 1 587.1 (diluted EPS) = 56.5%. **None equals 55.0%.** The 55.0% figure in D004 remains unexplained by any Note 9 denominator. Note: 897.0 ÷ 1 630.9 = 55.0% — the implied denominator 1 630.9c is still unidentified. **Remainder stays open** — flag as a D004/D005 reconciliation discrepancy (see new Q148).

**Q8 [MED] WANS gap and treasury shares**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Note 9 [D005.4, p. 62]: basic WANS 51 864k; diluted WANS 53 797k. Implied basic WANS from 853.8/16.462 = 51.87m ✓ matches 51 864k. Implied diluted WANS from 853.8/15.871 = 53.80m ✓ matches 53 797k. Dilution gap = 1 933k shares (3.7%), consistent with share-award dilution (share awards R72.6m, Q115). Shares in issue 51 646k vs basic WANS 51 864k — WANS exceeds shares in issue by 218k, consistent with treasury shares R(43.0)m held (Q119). Treasury share count still not explicitly stated.

**Q12 [HIGH] HE rand amount and HEPS reconciliation**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Note 9 [D005.4, p. 62]: Attributable earnings R853.8m + loss on disposal R0.5m + ROU impairment R47.5m + trademark impairment R7.6m = **Headline earnings R909.4m** (2025: R768.2m). HEPS 1 753.4c (2025: 1 482.5c) = +18.27%; HE +18.4% (909.4/768.2 = +18.38%) ✓. **Discrepancy flagged**: Note 9 capital items (0.5 + 47.5 + 7.6 = R55.6m pre-tax) differ from Note 8 total capital items R76.2m (ROU R65.1m + trademark R10.4m + disposal R0.7m) [D005.3, p. 61]. Note 9 uses **post-tax** amounts (ROU 65.1 × 0.73 ≈ 47.5 ✓; trademark 10.4 × 0.73 ≈ 7.6 ✓; disposal 0.7 × 0.73 ≈ 0.5 ✓). Reconciliation confirmed — Note 8 is pre-tax, Note 9 is post-tax. See new Q149.

**Q88 [HIGH] Note 9 HEPS reconciliation / post-tax add-back ≈ R55.6m**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Confirmed above. Post-tax add-back R55.6m bridges EPS 1 646.2c to HEPS 1 753.4c: 853.8 + 55.6 = 909.4; 909.4/51.864 = 1 753.4c ✓. Note 8 pre-tax R76.2m × (1 − 27%) = R55.6m ✓.

**Q89 [HIGH] Note 11 ETR reconciliation**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Note 11 [D005.4, p. 63]: PBT R1 134.9m; tax at 27% R306.4m; differing foreign rates +R4.6m; disallowances +R26.8m; exemptions −R63.6m; foreign withholding +R6.5m; prior years +R0.5m; tax rate change −R0.1m = **R281.1m**; ETR 24.8% (2025: 23.3%). Exemptions R63.6m (likely dividend/insurance-related) are the largest reconciling item. Prior-year adjustment now immaterial (+R0.5m vs −R21.7m). ETR below 27% statutory due to exemptions.

**Q129 [MED] Weighted average contractual interest rate 23.9% — EIR revenue consistency**
- **Status**: `Resolved`
- **Answer & evidence**: Note 12.1 [D005.4, p. 63]: "Included in cash flow from trading is interest earned on trade receivables of **R2 488.1m** (2025: R2 148.4m)." This is the gross interest earned on the debtors book, distinct from EIR revenue R2 383.4m (net of interest adjustment R104.7m per Note 2.2). 2 488.1 − 104.7 = 2 383.4 ✓ — **reconciles exactly**. Average book (9 167.3 + 7 958.0)/2 ≈ 8 562.7m; 2 488.1/8 562.7 = 29.1% gross yield vs 23.9% contractual (difference = fees/insurance/arrears interest). EIR revenue consistent.

### 2. Footnote & Qualification Deep-Dive

**Footnote [^1] — Note 10 segment assets** [D005.4, p. 62]
- Text: "Segment assets include net trade receivables of R5 708.7 million (2025: R4 996.6 million) and inventory of R768.7 million (2025: R765.9 million)."
- **Implication**: Segment assets R6 477.4m (2025: R5 762.5m) are dominated by the debtors book (88.1%) and inventory (11.9%). This confirms the group is effectively a credit-led retailer — segment asset growth +12.4% is almost entirely debtors book growth (+14.3% net). Inventory is essentially flat (+0.4%), confirming the inventory build slowdown seen in Note 12.2 (R13.5m vs R55.8m). **Forecast implication**: asset growth and hence capital absorption is a credit-book function, not a merchandise-inventory function. Must verify whether segment assets include ROU assets/PPE (they appear excluded — R6 477.4m vs total assets R9 159.1m leaves R2 681.7m for PPE, ROU, insurance investments, cash, deferred tax).

**Footnote [^1] — Note 10 geographical "BLE"** [D005.4, p. 62]
- Text: "Botswana, Lesotho and Eswatini."
- **Implication**: Cross-border revenue R1 505.7m (14.6% of group) split Namibia R776.7m + BLE R729.0m. BLE growth slowed to +4.1% (vs SA +11.8%, Namibia +10.0%) — BLE is the laggard. **Forecast implication**: cross-border growth decelerating; FX translation exposure on ~15% of revenue. Must verify FX rates and whether BLE slowdown is structural (consumer stress) or one-off.

**Note 9 — Dividends paid vs declared distinction** [D005.4, p. 62]
- Dividends **paid** 837.0c (final 2025 500.0c + interim 2026 337.0c); dividends **declared** 897.0c (interim 2026 337.0c + final 2026 560.0c).
- **Implication**: Final dividend 560.0c declared post-year-end is NOT in the FY26 cash flow (R435.8m paid) but IS in the D004 total dividend R469m. This resolves the Q67/Q110/Q114 gap: R469m declared vs R435.8m paid, difference ≈ R33.2m = final dividend 560.0c × ~5.9m shares? No — 560.0c × 51.646m = R289.2m, far larger. **Reconciliation tension**: D004 total dividend R469m cannot equal 897.0c × 51.646m = R463.3m exactly; and the R33.2m gap between R469m and R435.8m is NOT the final dividend (which is R289m). The R33.2m gap is more likely the difference between interim 2026 paid (337.0c) and interim 2025 paid (300.0c) timing, or shares entitled. **Flag as new Q151** — the D004 R469m figure needs re-examination; it may be a "dividends declared in respect of the year" figure (897.0c × 52.3m ≈ R469m) rather than cash paid.

**Note 9 — HEPS capital items are post-tax** [D005.4, p. 62]
- Note 9 add-backs: disposal R0.5m, ROU impairment R47.5m, trademark R7.6m = R55.6m. Note 8 pre-tax: R0.7m, R65.1m, R10.4m = R76.2m. Ratio 55.6/76.2 = 73.0% = (1 − 27%) ✓.
- **Implication**: Note 8 discloses pre-tax capital items; Note 9 discloses post-tax. No contradiction — but the two notes must be read together. **Forecast implication**: any future capital items should be tax-effected at 27% for HEPS purposes.

**Note 11 — Exemptions R63.6m** [D005.4, p. 63]
- Largest single reconciling item driving ETR to 24.8% (below 27%). Exemptions likely include dividend income, insurance-related exempt income, or B-BBEE/empowerment income.
- **Implication**: ETR is structurally below statutory. **Forecast implication**: model ETR at ~24.5–25.0%, not 27%. Must verify what the exemptions are (new Q152) — if they are non-recurring, ETR could normalise upward.

**Note 12.1 — Interest earned on trade receivables R2 488.1m** [D005.4, p. 63]
- "Included in cash flow from trading is interest earned on trade receivables of R2 488.1 million (2025: R2 148.4 million)."
- **Implication**: This is the gross interest yield on the debtors book, distinct from EIR revenue R2 383.4m. Difference R104.7m = interest adjustment per Note 2.2 (interest on credit-impaired debtors at amortised cost). **Reconciles exactly** — confirms EIR revenue is net of credit-impaired interest adjustment. **Forecast implication**: gross interest yield 2 488.1/8 562.7 avg book = 29.1%; EIR revenue yield 27.8%. Model EIR revenue as gross interest less credit-impaired adjustment.

**Note 12.2 — Working capital outflow R1 299.5m** [D005.4, p. 63]
- Trade receivables R(1 204.7)m (2025: R(999.1)m) — 92.7% of working capital outflow. Inventory R(13.5)m (2025: R(55.8)m) — build slowed. Trade payables R(47.9)m (2025: +R52.5m) — **payables reversed from inflow to outflow**, a R100.4m swing. Insurance contract asset R(40.7)m.
- **Implication**: Cash absorption is credit-book-driven and worsening. Payables outflow suggests either faster supplier payment or lower purchases (consistent with flat inventory). **Forecast implication**: working capital will remain a cash drag proportional to debtors book growth; payables outflow is a warning on supplier terms or demand.

**Note 12.3 — Interest paid R225.4m vs income statement R224.7m** [D005.4, p. 63]
- Other movements R(0.7)m (2025: R27.2m). **Implication**: cash interest ≈ accrual interest; no material timing difference in FY26 (unlike FY25 R27.2m). Confirms Note 5.3 R224.7m.

**Note 12.4 — Taxation paid R333.8m vs charge R265.1m** [D005.4, p. 63]
- Tax paid R333.8m (2025: R194.8m) vs normal tax + withholding charged R265.1m (2025: R257.6m). Opening payable R157.0m, closing payable R82.6m. **Implication**: tax paid exceeded charge by R68.7m due to settling opening payable. **Forecast implication**: FY27 tax paid should normalise toward the charge (~R280m) plus movement in payable.

**Note 12.5 — Real Beds acquisition** [D005.4, p. 63]
- Prior year (FY25): Real Beds R20.4m effective 29 June 2024 + four Botswana stores R15.3m effective 1 Nov 2024 = R35.7m total. "Immaterial impact."
- **Implication**: FY25 base includes ~9 months of Real Beds and ~5 months of Botswana stores — a small inorganic boost to FY25 that flatters FY26 growth comparisons slightly. **Forecast implication**: FY26 organic growth is marginally below reported; immaterial but note for like-for-like.

**Note 12.6 — Lease liability payments R494.4m** [D005.4, p. 63]
- Total R494.4m (2025: R456.3m), capital portion R328.4m (2025: R296.7m) in financing, remaining R166.0m in cash flow from trading (interest). **Implication**: lease interest R166.0m vs Note 5.3 lease finance charge R90.4m — **discrepancy of R75.6m**. The R166.0m "remaining balance in cash flow from trading" may include short-term/low-value lease payments expensed directly, not just IFRS 16 interest. **Flag as new Q153** — reconcile R166.0m to Note 5.3 R90.4m lease finance charge.

**Note 13 — No significant post-balance-sheet events** [D005.4, p. 63]
- **Implication**: No post-year-end trading update, no acquisitions/disposals, no credit events disclosed. **Forecast implication**: FY27 starts from a clean base with no disclosed shocks; but also no post-year-end collection data (Q125 remains open).
